Spring Security · Procurement Information System

PIS Security Course

From a wide-open API to one account per person, signed tokens, refresh tokens, permissions, roles managed in the database, a separate OAuth2 authorization server, and finally services that find each other through Eureka behind an API gateway. Every lesson builds on real PIS code, and every lesson ends with a smoke-test script you can run against your own app.

1Basic→ 2Users→ 3JWT→ 3BRefresh→ 3CPermissions→ 3DRoles in DB→ 4OAuth2→ 5Gateway→ 6Keycloak→ 7Social

Lesson 1 · 2 hours

Who are you, and what may you do?

Lock the API with HTTP Basic authentication and role rules, then prove it with curl, Swagger UI and tests.

ProjectPIS project Markdowndocs/lessons/01-basic-auth.md Check itStep by step · Smoke test

GOALSBy the end of this lesson you can…

  • Explain the difference between authentication and authorization, and between 401 and 403.
  • Read and write an HTTP Basic Authorization header by hand, and explain why Basic auth needs HTTPS.
  • Configure a SecurityFilterChain with role rules and explain why rule order matters.
  • Store passwords as BCrypt hashes, not plain text.
  • Test security with httpBasic() and @WithMockUser.
TimeActivity
0–15Concepts: authentication vs authorization, how Basic works
15–25How a request passes through the security filters
25–60Build it: dependency, users, rules, error handler, Swagger
60–75Try it live with curl and Swagger UI
75–95Test it: SecurityTest walkthrough
95–120Exercises and quiz

CONCEPTSTwo questions, asked in order

Authentication

Who are you? Prove your identity: username and password. When it fails, the answer is 401 Unauthorized.

Authorization

What may you do? Check your role against the rule for this URL. When it fails, the answer is 403 Forbidden.

Remember it like this401 means "I don't know who you are." 403 means "I know exactly who you are, and the answer is no." (The name "Unauthorized" for 401 is a historical mistake in HTTP. It really means unauthenticated.)

How HTTP Basic works

  1. The client calls GET /api/v1/suppliers with no credentials.
  2. The server replies 401 with WWW-Authenticate: Basic realm="pis". That header says: "use Basic auth".
  3. The client joins username and password with a colon, Base64-encodes it, and sends it again: Authorization: Basic …
  4. The server decodes it, looks up the user, checks the password against the stored hash, then checks the role rules.
  5. There is no session and no login endpoint. The client sends the header on every single request.
Base64 is not encryptionAnyone who sees the header can decode it in one command, as the Try it section shows. Basic auth is safe only over HTTPS.

Where passwords are stored

The server never stores the password itself. It stores a BCrypt hash: a one-way value. At login, Spring hashes what you typed and compares the two hashes.

A stored password looks like this
{bcrypt}$2a$10$N9qo8uLOickgx2ZMRZoMyeIjZAgcfl7p92ldGxad68LJZdL17lhWy

The {bcrypt} prefix names the algorithm, so you can switch to a stronger one later while old passwords keep working.

Roles in PIS

Procurement separates duties: the person who requests a purchase should not be the one who approves it.

UserRolesJob
officerOFFICERRegisters suppliers, raises requisitions and purchase orders
approverAPPROVERApproves or rejects requisitions, changes supplier status
adminADMIN, APPROVER, OFFICEREverything, including deletes

This is RBAC: role-based access control

What you're building has a name: role-based access control (RBAC). Every rule names a role, and a person is allowed in if they hold it: hasRole("APPROVER"). It's the right starting point, simple and close to how an organisation thinks ("approvers approve"). It has two limits, and Lesson 3C fixes both:

LimitExample in PISFixed by
The rule names a job title, not an actionAdding a read-only auditor means checking every rule by handPermissions: hasAuthority("requisition:approve"), with roles as bundles of permissions
The rule can't look at the recordAn approver who raised a requisition could approve it themselves@PreAuthorize with a rule that checks created_by
TermQuestion it answersSpring
Role-based (RBAC)Is this person an APPROVER?hasRole("APPROVER")
Permission-basedMay this person approve requisitions?hasAuthority("requisition:approve")
Attribute-based (ABAC)May this person approve this requisition?@PreAuthorize("… @requisitionGuard.raisedBy(#id, authentication.name)")

REQUEST FLOWSecurity runs before your controller

Spring Security is a chain of servlet filters. A rejected request never touches a controller or a service.

sequenceDiagram
    participant C as Client (curl)
    participant B as BasicAuthenticationFilter
    participant A as AuthorizationFilter
    participant D as Controller
    C->>B: POST /requisitions/{id}/approve + Authorization header
    alt header missing or password wrong
        B-->>C: 401 via ProblemDetailSecurityHandler.commence()
    else credentials valid
        B->>A: authenticated as officer (ROLE_OFFICER)
        alt role not allowed by the rules
            A-->>C: 403 via ProblemDetailSecurityHandler.handle()
        else role allowed
            A->>D: request reaches RequisitionController
            D-->>C: 200 / 404 / 422 via GlobalExceptionHandler
        end
    end
Why we need a second error handlerGlobalExceptionHandler only sees exceptions thrown inside controllers. A 401 or 403 happens in a filter, before any controller runs, so ProblemDetailSecurityHandler gives those errors the same JSON shape.

BUILD ITSix changes, one at a time

1 · Add the dependencies

The moment this starter is on the classpath, every endpoint is locked, with a generated user called user and a random password in the log. Show this before writing any config.

pom.xml
<dependency>
    <groupId>org.springframework.boot</groupId>
    <artifactId>spring-boot-starter-security</artifactId>
</dependency>

<!-- test scope: httpBasic(), @WithMockUser -->
<dependency>
    <groupId>org.springframework.security</groupId>
    <artifactId>spring-security-test</artifactId>
    <scope>test</scope>
</dependency>

2 · Keep passwords out of the code

src/main/resources/application.yml
pis:
  security:
    officer-password: ${OFFICER_PASSWORD}
    approver-password: ${APPROVER_PASSWORD}
    admin-password: ${ADMIN_PASSWORD}
.env (never committed)
OFFICER_PASSWORD={{OFFICER}}
APPROVER_PASSWORD={{APPROVER}}
ADMIN_PASSWORD={{ADMIN}}

3 · The users: authentication

config/SecurityConfig.java
@Bean
public UserDetailsService userDetailsService(
        PasswordEncoder encoder,
        @Value("${pis.security.officer-password}") String officerPassword,
        @Value("${pis.security.approver-password}") String approverPassword,
        @Value("${pis.security.admin-password}") String adminPassword) {

    // roles("OFFICER") stores the authority "ROLE_OFFICER"; hasRole("OFFICER") checks for it.
    return new InMemoryUserDetailsManager(
            User.withUsername("officer")
                    .password(encoder.encode(officerPassword))
                    .roles("OFFICER")
                    .build(),
            User.withUsername("approver")
                    .password(encoder.encode(approverPassword))
                    .roles("APPROVER")
                    .build(),
            User.withUsername("admin")
                    .password(encoder.encode(adminPassword))
                    .roles("ADMIN", "APPROVER", "OFFICER")
                    .build());
}

@Bean
public PasswordEncoder passwordEncoder() {
    return PasswordEncoderFactories.createDelegatingPasswordEncoder();   // BCrypt by default
}

4 · The rules: authorization

config/SecurityConfig.java
@Bean
public SecurityFilterChain filterChain(HttpSecurity http) throws Exception {
    return http
            // No session cookie, so there is nothing for CSRF to forge.
            .csrf(AbstractHttpConfigurer::disable)
            .sessionManagement(s -> s.sessionCreationPolicy(SessionCreationPolicy.STATELESS))

            // Checked top to bottom. The FIRST match wins.
            .authorizeHttpRequests(auth -> auth
                    .requestMatchers("/actuator/health", "/swagger-ui.html", "/swagger-ui/**",
                            "/v3/api-docs/**").permitAll()

                    .requestMatchers(HttpMethod.POST, "/api/v1/requisitions/*/approve",
                            "/api/v1/requisitions/*/reject").hasRole("APPROVER")
                    .requestMatchers(HttpMethod.PATCH, "/api/v1/suppliers/*/status").hasRole("APPROVER")

                    .requestMatchers(HttpMethod.DELETE, "/api/v1/**").hasRole("ADMIN")
                    .requestMatchers(HttpMethod.GET, "/api/v1/**").authenticated()
                    .requestMatchers("/api/v1/**").hasRole("OFFICER")

                    .anyRequest().denyAll())

            .httpBasic(basic -> basic.authenticationEntryPoint(problemHandler))
            .exceptionHandling(ex -> ex
                    .authenticationEntryPoint(problemHandler)   // 401
                    .accessDeniedHandler(problemHandler))       // 403
            .build();
}
RequestAllowed
Health check, Swagger UI, API docsAnyone
POST …/requisitions/{id}/approve and /rejectAPPROVER
PATCH …/suppliers/{id}/statusAPPROVER
Any DELETEADMIN
Any GETAny logged-in user
Any other write (POST, PUT)OFFICER
Anything not listedNobody
Order mattersMove .requestMatchers("/api/v1/**").hasRole("OFFICER") to the top and it matches every API call first: approvers are refused on approve, and deletes only need OFFICER.

5 · 401 and 403 as problem details

config/ProblemDetailSecurityHandler.java (core)
@Override   // 401: no credentials, or wrong ones
public void commence(HttpServletRequest request, HttpServletResponse response,
                     AuthenticationException ex) throws IOException {
    response.setHeader(HttpHeaders.WWW_AUTHENTICATE, "Basic realm=\"pis\"");
    write(response, HttpStatus.UNAUTHORIZED, "Authentication required",
            "Send a valid username and password using HTTP Basic", "unauthorized");
}

@Override   // 403: valid user, wrong role
public void handle(HttpServletRequest request, HttpServletResponse response,
                   AccessDeniedException ex) throws IOException {
    write(response, HttpStatus.FORBIDDEN, "Access denied",
            "Your role does not permit this operation", "forbidden");
}

6 · The Authorize button in Swagger UI

config/OpenApiConfig.java
.components(new Components().addSecuritySchemes("basicAuth",
        new SecurityScheme().type(SecurityScheme.Type.HTTP).scheme("basic")))
.addSecurityItem(new SecurityRequirement().addList("basicAuth"));

TRY ITLive demo with curl

Start the app with mvn spring-boot:run. Before each command, ask the class to predict the status code.

No credentials

terminal
curl -i {{BASE}}/api/v1/suppliers
401 WWW-Authenticate: Basic realm="pis" {"type":"https://hmy.co.tz/problems/unauthorized","title":"Authentication required","status":401,...}

Wrong password

terminal
curl -s -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code}\n' -u officer:wrong {{BASE}}/api/v1/suppliers
401

Correct credentials: -u builds the header for you

terminal
curl -s -u 'officer:{{OFFICER}}' {{BASE}}/api/v1/suppliers | jq
200 {"content":[{"name":"Example Office Supplies Ltd", ...}], "page":0, ...}

The same request, header built by hand

terminal
printf 'officer:{{OFFICER}}' | base64          # {{OFFICER_B64}}
curl -s -H "Authorization: Basic {{OFFICER_B64}}" {{BASE}}/api/v1/suppliers | jq

echo {{OFFICER_B64}} | base64 -d     # anyone can do this

Right person, wrong role

terminal
curl -s -u 'officer:{{OFFICER}}' -X POST \
  {{BASE}}/api/v1/requisitions/99999999-9999-9999-9999-999999999999/approve | jq
403 {"title":"Access denied","detail":"Your role does not permit this operation",...}

Right role

terminal
curl -s -u 'approver:{{APPROVER}}' -X POST \
  {{BASE}}/api/v1/requisitions/99999999-9999-9999-9999-999999999999/approve | jq
404 {"title":"Resource not found","detail":"Requisition not found: 9999…",...}
Why is 404 a success here?The service only runs if security let the request through. A 404 proves the approver passed both checks, without changing any data.

Swagger UI

  1. Open /swagger-ui.html. It loads without a password, because it is on the permitAll() list.
  2. Try GET /api/v1/suppliers. You get 401.
  3. Click Authorize, log in as officer, and try again. You get 200.
  4. Try DELETE /api/v1/suppliers/{id}. You get 403. Authorize as admin, and try again.

To check every rule in one go, run the Lesson 1 smoke test.

TEST ITTwo tools, two purposes

httpBasic("user", "pass")

Sends a real Authorization header, so the password check runs. Tests authentication.

@WithMockUser(roles = "…")

Puts a user straight into the security context and skips the password. Tests authorization rules.

Tests use fixed, fake passwords from application-test.yml, so they pass on every machine.

SecurityTest.java (extract)
@Test
void no_credentials_returns_401_with_a_basic_challenge() throws Exception {
    mvc.perform(get("/api/v1/suppliers"))
        .andExpect(status().isUnauthorized())
        .andExpect(header().string(HttpHeaders.WWW_AUTHENTICATE, "Basic realm=\"pis\""))
        .andExpect(jsonPath("$.title").value("Authentication required"));
}

@Test
void correct_credentials_return_200() throws Exception {
    mvc.perform(get("/api/v1/suppliers").with(httpBasic("officer", "officer-pass")))
        .andExpect(status().isOk());
}

@Test
@WithMockUser(roles = "OFFICER")
void officer_cannot_approve_a_requisition() throws Exception {
    mvc.perform(post("/api/v1/requisitions/{id}/approve", UNKNOWN_ID))
        .andExpect(status().isForbidden());
}

@Test
@WithMockUser(roles = "APPROVER")
void approver_can_approve_a_requisition() throws Exception {
    mvc.perform(post("/api/v1/requisitions/{id}/approve", UNKNOWN_ID))
        .andExpect(status().isNotFound());   // got past security
}

Existing tests need a user now. One class-level annotation fixes SupplierApiTest: @WithMockUser(roles = "OFFICER").

mvn test → Tests run: 21, Failures: 0, Errors: 0, Skipped: 0

MISTAKESWhat usually goes wrong

Every POST returns 403, even for admin

CSRF protection is still on. For a stateless API with Basic auth, disable it: .csrf(AbstractHttpConfigurer::disable). Keep it on for browser apps with session cookies.

hasRole("ROLE_ADMIN") fails at startup

hasRole adds the ROLE_ prefix itself. Write hasRole("ADMIN"), or hasAuthority("ROLE_ADMIN").

A rule seems to be ignored

A broader matcher above it matched first. Move specific paths above general ones.

"Could not resolve placeholder 'OFFICER_PASSWORD'"

Your .env is missing the password variables. Copy them from .env.example.

The browser keeps showing a login pop-up

That is the browser reacting to WWW-Authenticate: Basic. It then caches the credentials until you close it: Basic auth has no real logout.

"There is no PasswordEncoder mapped for the id null"

A password was stored without an {id} prefix. Always pass it through encoder.encode(...).

EXERCISESNow you try

1 · Decode a captured header Warm-up

You capture Authorization: Basic YXBwcm92ZXI6YXBwcm92ZXIxMjM= from an unencrypted request. Who is the user, what is the password, and which role do they have?

Solution

echo YXBwcm92ZXI6YXBwcm92ZXIxMjM= | base64 -d gives approver:approver123. The role is APPROVER.

2 · Add a read-only auditor Core

Add a user auditor with role AUDITOR. The auditor can read everything and change nothing. Test: can list suppliers (200), cannot create one (403).

Solution

No new rule is needed: GET is allowed for any authenticated user, and every write needs another role. Add the user, the yml property, the .env variable, and a fake password in application-test.yml.

3 · Only approvers may issue purchase orders Core

Make POST /api/v1/purchase-orders/{id}/issue need APPROVER. Prove it with a 403 test for OFFICER and a 404 test for APPROVER.

Solution
.requestMatchers(HttpMethod.POST, "/api/v1/requisitions/*/approve",
        "/api/v1/requisitions/*/reject",
        "/api/v1/purchase-orders/*/issue").hasRole("APPROVER")

4 · Break it on purpose Understanding

Move .requestMatchers("/api/v1/**").hasRole("OFFICER") to the top and run mvn test. Which tests fail, and why?

Solution

Four tests fail. approver_can_approve_a_requisition and any_authenticated_user_can_read get 403, because an approver is not an officer. officer_cannot_approve_a_requisition and officer_cannot_delete get 404 instead of 403, because the officer now reaches the service. The first match wins.

5 · Prove the password is hashed Stretch

Encode "secret" twice. Assert the hashes differ, both start with {bcrypt}, and both match. Why do they differ?

Solution
PasswordEncoder encoder = PasswordEncoderFactories.createDelegatingPasswordEncoder();
String a = encoder.encode("secret");
String b = encoder.encode("secret");

assertThat(a).isNotEqualTo(b).startsWith("{bcrypt}");
assertThat(encoder.matches("secret", a)).isTrue();
assertThat(encoder.matches("secret", b)).isTrue();

BCrypt adds a random salt to every hash, so the same password never gives the same hash twice.

QUIZCheck yourself

1. An officer calls DELETE /api/v1/suppliers/{id} with the correct password. What comes back?

The officer is authenticated, so it is not 401. DELETE needs ADMIN, so it is 403.

2. What does Base64 do to the credentials in a Basic header?

Base64 is a reversible encoding, not security. HTTPS is what protects the header.

3. Why can't GlobalExceptionHandler produce our 401 and 403 responses?

@RestControllerAdvice only sees exceptions thrown from controllers.

4. Which test tool checks that the password itself is verified?

@WithMockUser skips the password check. httpBasic() sends a real header.

5. Why is it safe to disable CSRF in this API?

CSRF tricks a browser into sending a cookie it holds. A stateless API has no session cookie to abuse.

SMOKE TESTCheck the whole lesson in one go

Checks every rule from this lesson. Changes no data. Save it as a file and run bash smoke.sh while the app is up. Each step prints PASS or FAIL with the status it got.

Needs the officer, approver and admin passwords from your .env. Changes no data. After Lesson 2 it still works, once the smoke test below has created officer and approver.

smoke-lesson1.sh
#!/usr/bin/env bash
# Lesson 1 smoke test: HTTP Basic and role rules.
BASE={{BASE}}
ID=99999999-9999-9999-9999-999999999999   # does not exist: 404 means "got past security"

check() {   # check <expected status> <description> <curl args...>
  local want=$1 desc=$2; shift 2
  local got
  got=$(curl -s -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code}' "$@")
  if [ "$got" = "$want" ]; then echo "PASS  $got  $desc"; else echo "FAIL  $got  $desc (expected $want)"; fi
}

check 200 "health check is public"             "$BASE/actuator/health"
check 401 "no credentials"                     "$BASE/api/v1/suppliers"
check 401 "wrong password"                     -u officer:wrong "$BASE/api/v1/suppliers"
check 200 "officer can read"                   -u 'officer:{{OFFICER}}' "$BASE/api/v1/suppliers"
check 403 "officer cannot approve"             -u 'officer:{{OFFICER}}' -X POST "$BASE/api/v1/requisitions/$ID/approve"
check 404 "approver can approve"               -u 'approver:{{APPROVER}}' -X POST "$BASE/api/v1/requisitions/$ID/approve"
check 403 "approver cannot create a supplier"  -u 'approver:{{APPROVER}}' -X POST -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d '{}' "$BASE/api/v1/suppliers"
check 403 "officer cannot delete"              -u 'officer:{{OFFICER}}' -X DELETE "$BASE/api/v1/suppliers/$ID"
check 404 "admin can delete"                   -u 'admin:{{ADMIN}}' -X DELETE "$BASE/api/v1/suppliers/$ID"
PASS 200 health check is public PASS 401 no credentials PASS 401 wrong password PASS 200 officer can read PASS 403 officer cannot approve PASS 404 approver can approve PASS 403 approver cannot create a supplier PASS 403 officer cannot delete PASS 404 admin can delete

Lesson 2 · 2½ hours

Users in the database, and who did what

Lesson 1 locked the door. Lesson 2 hands out the keys, and writes down who used them.

ProjectPIS project Markdowndocs/lessons/02-users-in-database.md Check itStep by step · Smoke test

GOALSBy the end of this lesson you can…

  • Explain what a UserDetailsService does and write one backed by a JPA repository.
  • Design app_user and app_user_role tables with a Flyway migration.
  • Hash a password when an account is created, and never return it.
  • Solve the first admin problem without putting a password in git.
  • Disable an account instead of deleting it, and explain why.
  • Fill created_by and updated_by automatically with AuditorAware.
TimeActivity
0–15Why in-memory users are not enough; the UserDetailsService contract
15–25Request flow: from the Basic header to a row in app_user
25–70Build it, part A: tables, entity, DatabaseUserDetailsService, first admin
70–95Build it, part B: the users API
95–110Build it, part C: who did it
110–125Try it live with curl and psql
125–150Tests, exercises, quiz

CONCEPTSOne account per person

In memory (Lesson 1)In the database (Lesson 2)
Adding a user means editing Java and redeployingAn admin calls POST /api/v1/users
Can't disable one person without a releasePATCH /users/{id}/enabled takes effect on their next request
Three shared logins: nobody knows which officer did somethingOne account per person, so created_by means something

The last row is the real reason. An audit trail is only as good as the accounts behind it.

The UserDetailsService contract

Spring Security doesn't know your tables. It asks one question through one interface:

Spring Security
public interface UserDetailsService {
    UserDetails loadUserByUsername(String username) throws UsernameNotFoundException;
}

You return the username, the stored hash, the roles, and whether the account is enabled. Spring compares the password itself. Your code never compares passwords. Swap Lesson 1's in-memory implementation for a database one and the rules don't change.

Two tables, not one

app_user app_user_role ┌──────────┬───────────────┬─────────┐ ┌──────────┬──────────┐ │ username │ password_hash │ enabled │ │ user_id │ role │ ├──────────┼───────────────┼─────────┤ ├──────────┼──────────┤ │ admin │ {bcrypt}$2a$… │ true │◄────┤ admin-id │ ADMIN │ │ officer │ {bcrypt}$2a$… │ true │ │ admin-id │ APPROVER │ └──────────┴───────────────┴─────────┘ │ admin-id │ OFFICER │ │ off-id │ OFFICER │ └──────────┴──────────┘
Why app_user and not user?USER is a reserved word in PostgreSQL. SELECT * FROM user returns the current database user, not your table.

The first-admin problem

OptionProblem
Insert the admin in a Flyway migrationThe password hash is in git forever, identical on every install
Let anyone call POST /users while the table is emptyA race: whoever gets there first owns the system
On startup, if app_user is empty, create admin from ADMIN_PASSWORD✅ The secret stays in .env. Runs once, then never again

Disable, don't delete

Deleting a user who created 300 purchase orders would leave 300 records pointing at nobody. Procurement records are evidence. enabled = false makes Spring reject the login with 401, and the name stays on everything that person did.

Who did it: AuditorAware

SecurityContextHolder holds the user that BasicAuthenticationFilter authenticated for this request, on this thread. When a service calls save(), Spring Data asks AuditorAware for that name and writes it to created_by. The client never sends it, so the client can't lie about it.

REQUEST FLOWFrom Basic header to created_by

sequenceDiagram
    participant C as Client
    participant F as BasicAuthenticationFilter
    participant P as DaoAuthenticationProvider
    participant U as DatabaseUserDetailsService
    participant DB as PostgreSQL
    participant S as SupplierService
    participant A as AuditorAware
    C->>F: POST /suppliers + Authorization: Basic ...
    F->>P: authenticate("officer", password)
    P->>U: loadUserByUsername("officer")
    U->>DB: SELECT from app_user / app_user_role
    DB-->>U: hash, roles [OFFICER], enabled
    U-->>P: UserDetails
    P->>P: passwordEncoder.matches(password, hash)
    alt wrong password, unknown user, or disabled
        P-->>C: 401
    else ok
        P-->>F: Authentication stored in SecurityContextHolder
        F->>S: request continues to the controller and service
        S->>A: save(supplier): who is the current user?
        A-->>S: "officer"
        S->>DB: INSERT supplier (created_by = 'officer')
    end
DaoAuthenticationProviderSpring Boot creates it automatically when it finds exactly one UserDetailsService bean and one PasswordEncoder bean.

BUILD ITPart A: users in the database

1 · The migration

db/migration/V4__create_app_user.sql
-- The table is app_user, not user: USER is a reserved word in PostgreSQL.
-- password_hash holds a BCrypt hash with its {bcrypt} prefix, never a password.

CREATE TABLE app_user (
    id            UUID          PRIMARY KEY,
    username      VARCHAR(50)   NOT NULL,
    password_hash VARCHAR(100)  NOT NULL,
    full_name     VARCHAR(150)  NOT NULL,
    enabled       BOOLEAN       NOT NULL DEFAULT TRUE,
    created_at    TIMESTAMPTZ   NOT NULL,
    updated_at    TIMESTAMPTZ,
    created_by    VARCHAR(50),
    updated_by    VARCHAR(50),
    CONSTRAINT uk_app_user_username UNIQUE (username)
);

-- One row per role. A composite key stops the same role being granted twice.
CREATE TABLE app_user_role (
    user_id UUID        NOT NULL REFERENCES app_user (id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
    role    VARCHAR(20) NOT NULL,
    PRIMARY KEY (user_id, role)
);

2 · The entity

model/Role.java
public enum Role {
    OFFICER,
    APPROVER,
    ADMIN
}
model/AppUser.java
@Entity
@Table(name = "app_user", uniqueConstraints =
        @UniqueConstraint(name = "uk_app_user_username", columnNames = "username"))
@Data
@EqualsAndHashCode(callSuper = true)
@NoArgsConstructor(access = AccessLevel.PROTECTED)
@ToString(of = { "id", "username" }) // never the password hash, not even in a log line
public class AppUser extends Auditable {

    @Id
    @GeneratedValue(strategy = GenerationType.UUID)
    private UUID id;

    /** Always stored in lower case, so "Officer" and "officer" are the same account. */
    @Column(nullable = false, length = 50)
    private String username;

    @Column(name = "password_hash", nullable = false, length = 100)
    private String passwordHash;

    @Column(name = "full_name", nullable = false, length = 150)
    private String fullName;

    @Column(nullable = false)
    private boolean enabled = true;

    @ElementCollection
    @CollectionTable(name = "app_user_role", joinColumns = @JoinColumn(name = "user_id"))
    @Enumerated(EnumType.STRING)
    @Column(name = "role", nullable = false, length = 20)
    private Set<Role> roles = new HashSet<>();

    public AppUser(String username, String passwordHash, String fullName, Set<Role> roles) {
        this.username = username.toLowerCase();
        this.passwordHash = passwordHash;
        this.fullName = fullName;
        this.roles = new HashSet<>(roles);
    }
}
repository/AppUserRepository.java
public interface AppUserRepository extends JpaRepository<AppUser, UUID> {

    Optional<AppUser> findByUsername(String username);

    boolean existsByUsername(String username);
}

3 · DatabaseUserDetailsService

service/DatabaseUserDetailsService.java
@RequiredArgsConstructor
@Service
public class DatabaseUserDetailsService implements UserDetailsService {

    private final AppUserRepository users;

    @Override
    @Transactional(readOnly = true) // roles are lazy; read them while the session is open
    public UserDetails loadUserByUsername(String username) {
        AppUser user = users.findByUsername(username.toLowerCase())
                // Same 401 as a wrong password: never tell a caller which usernames exist.
                .orElseThrow(() -> new UsernameNotFoundException("Bad credentials"));

        return User.withUsername(user.getUsername())
                .password(user.getPasswordHash())
                .roles(user.getRoles().stream().map(Enum::name).toArray(String[]::new))
                .disabled(!user.isEnabled())
                .build();
    }
}
Why @Transactional?roles loads lazily and this project runs with open-in-view: false. Without a transaction, getRoles() throws LazyInitializationException.

4 · Remove the in-memory users

In SecurityConfig, delete the whole userDetailsService(...) bean. Keep passwordEncoder().

Leave it in and logins stop workingWith two UserDetailsService beans the log says Found 2 UserDetailsService beans … Global Authentication Manager will not use a UserDetailsService, and every request gets 401.

5 · The first admin

config/AdminAccountInitializer.java
/**
 * Only an admin can create users, so who creates the first admin?
 * On startup, if app_user is empty, create one admin from ADMIN_PASSWORD.
 * Once any user exists this does nothing.
 */
@Slf4j
@RequiredArgsConstructor
@Component
public class AdminAccountInitializer implements ApplicationRunner {

    private final AppUserRepository users;
    private final PasswordEncoder passwordEncoder;

    @Value("${pis.security.admin-password}")
    private String adminPassword;

    @Override
    @Transactional
    public void run(ApplicationArguments args) {
        if (users.count() > 0) {
            return;
        }
        users.save(new AppUser("admin", passwordEncoder.encode(adminPassword), "System Administrator",
                Set.of(Role.ADMIN, Role.APPROVER, Role.OFFICER)));
        log.warn("No users found. Created the initial 'admin' account from ADMIN_PASSWORD.");
    }
}
application.yml
# Password for the first admin, created on startup only while app_user is empty.
pis:
  security:
    admin-password: ${ADMIN_PASSWORD}
.env: delete OFFICER_PASSWORD and APPROVER_PASSWORD, keep
ADMIN_PASSWORD={{ADMIN}}

Part B · 6 · Request and response

dto/UserRequest.java
public record UserRequest(

        @NotBlank(message = "username is required")
        @Pattern(regexp = "[a-zA-Z0-9._-]{3,50}",
                 message = "username must be 3-50 letters, digits, dots, dashes or underscores")
        String username,

        // BCrypt ignores everything after 72 bytes.
        @NotBlank(message = "password is required")
        @Size(min = 8, max = 72, message = "password must be 8-72 characters")
        String password,

        @NotBlank(message = "full name is required")
        @Size(max = 150)
        String fullName,

        @NotEmpty(message = "at least one role is required")
        Set<Role> roles
) { }
dto/UserResponse.java
/** No password and no hash. There is no reason for either to ever leave the server. */
public record UserResponse(
        UUID id,
        String username,
        String fullName,
        boolean enabled,
        Set<Role> roles,
        Instant createdAt,
        String createdBy
) {
    public static UserResponse from(AppUser u) {
        return new UserResponse(u.getId(), u.getUsername(), u.getFullName(), u.isEnabled(),
                Set.copyOf(u.getRoles()), u.getCreatedAt(), u.getCreatedBy());
    }
}
dto/UserEnabledRequest.java
public record UserEnabledRequest(@NotNull(message = "enabled is required") Boolean enabled) { }

7 · The service

service/UserService.java
@RequiredArgsConstructor
@Service
@Transactional(readOnly = true)
public class UserService {

    private final AppUserRepository users;
    private final PasswordEncoder passwordEncoder;

    @Transactional
    public UserResponse create(UserRequest request) {
        String username = request.username().toLowerCase();
        if (users.existsByUsername(username)) {
            throw new DuplicateResourceException("Username " + username + " is already taken");
        }
        // The only place a plain password exists: it is hashed before it reaches the entity.
        AppUser user = new AppUser(username, passwordEncoder.encode(request.password()),
                request.fullName(), request.roles());
        return UserResponse.from(users.save(user));
    }

    public List<UserResponse> findAll() {
        return users.findAll().stream().map(UserResponse::from).toList();
    }

    public UserResponse findByUsername(String username) {
        return users.findByUsername(username)
                .map(UserResponse::from)
                .orElseThrow(() -> new ResourceNotFoundException("User", username));
    }

    /** Disable, don't delete: the user's name stays on every record they created. */
    @Transactional
    public UserResponse setEnabled(UUID id, boolean enabled) {
        AppUser user = users.findById(id)
                .orElseThrow(() -> new ResourceNotFoundException("User", id));
        user.setEnabled(enabled);
        return UserResponse.from(user);
    }
}

8 · The controller

controller/UserController.java
@Tag(name = "Users", description = "Accounts that can log in")
@RequiredArgsConstructor
@RestController
@RequestMapping("/api/v1/users")
public class UserController {

    private final UserService service;

    @GetMapping("/me")
    public UserResponse me(@AuthenticationPrincipal UserDetails principal) {
        return service.findByUsername(principal.getUsername());
    }

    @PostMapping
    public ResponseEntity<UserResponse> create(@Valid @RequestBody UserRequest request,
                                               UriComponentsBuilder uri) {
        UserResponse created = service.create(request);
        return ResponseEntity
                .created(uri.path("/api/v1/users/{id}").buildAndExpand(created.id()).toUri())
                .body(created);
    }

    @GetMapping
    public List<UserResponse> findAll() {
        return service.findAll();
    }

    @PatchMapping("/{id}/enabled")
    public UserResponse setEnabled(@PathVariable UUID id,
                                   @Valid @RequestBody UserEnabledRequest request) {
        return service.setEnabled(id, request.enabled());
    }
}

@AuthenticationPrincipal hands the controller the UserDetails for this request. No need to read the header yourself.

9 · Two new security rules

SecurityConfig: above the general GET rule
// Anyone logged in may ask who they are; only admins manage accounts.
// Both sit above the general GET rule, or that rule would match first.
.requestMatchers(HttpMethod.GET, "/api/v1/users/me").authenticated()
.requestMatchers("/api/v1/users/**").hasRole("ADMIN")

/api/v1/users/** also matches /api/v1/users: ** means zero or more path segments.

Part C · 10 · The audit columns

db/migration/V5__add_audit_user_columns.sql
-- Nullable: rows that existed before this migration have no known author.
-- A username, not a foreign key: the audit trail must survive a user being deleted.

ALTER TABLE supplier       ADD COLUMN created_by VARCHAR(50), ADD COLUMN updated_by VARCHAR(50);
ALTER TABLE requisition    ADD COLUMN created_by VARCHAR(50), ADD COLUMN updated_by VARCHAR(50);
ALTER TABLE purchase_order ADD COLUMN created_by VARCHAR(50), ADD COLUMN updated_by VARCHAR(50);
ALTER TABLE invoice        ADD COLUMN created_by VARCHAR(50), ADD COLUMN updated_by VARCHAR(50);
A new migration, never an edit to V1Flyway stores a checksum of every migration it has run. Edit V1 and the app refuses to start on every database that already ran it.

11 · Auditable learns the user

model/Auditable.java: add below updatedAt
/** Username from the SecurityContext, supplied by JpaConfig.currentUser(). */
@CreatedBy
@Column(name = "created_by", updatable = false, length = 50)
private String createdBy;

@LastModifiedBy
@Column(name = "updated_by", length = 50)
private String updatedBy;

12 · AuditorAware: where the name comes from

config/JpaConfig.java
@Configuration
@EnableJpaAuditing(auditorAwareRef = "currentUser")
public class JpaConfig {

    /** Who is making this change? Spring Data calls this on every insert and update. */
    @Bean
    public AuditorAware<String> currentUser() {
        return () -> Optional.ofNullable(SecurityContextHolder.getContext().getAuthentication())
                .filter(Authentication::isAuthenticated)
                .filter(auth -> !(auth instanceof AnonymousAuthenticationToken))
                .map(Authentication::getName)
                .or(() -> Optional.of("system"));   // e.g. AdminAccountInitializer at startup
    }
}

13 · Show it in the response

dto/SupplierResponse.java
        Instant createdAt,
        Instant updatedAt,
        String createdBy,
        String updatedBy
) {
    public static SupplierResponse from(Supplier s) {
        return new SupplierResponse(
                // ... existing values ...
                s.getCreatedAt(), s.getUpdatedAt(),
                s.getCreatedBy(), s.getUpdatedBy());
    }
}

The request never mentioned createdBy. Add "createdBy":"admin" to the JSON: it is ignored, because SupplierRequest has no such field.

Look inside the database

terminal
psql -d pmis -c "SELECT u.username, u.password_hash, r.role
                 FROM app_user u JOIN app_user_role r ON r.user_id = u.id ORDER BY 1;"
username | password_hash | role ----------+-----------------------------------------------------------------------+---------- admin | {bcrypt}$2a$10$AG0NKdiFlmYddFl7qqXeluiENByVYrfzYqSyY3wSs105h3uhohCny | ADMIN admin | {bcrypt}$2a$10$AG0NKdiFlmYddFl7qqXeluiENByVYrfzYqSyY3wSs105h3uhohCny | APPROVER admin | {bcrypt}$2a$10$AG0NKdiFlmYddFl7qqXeluiENByVYrfzYqSyY3wSs105h3uhohCny | OFFICER officer | {bcrypt}$2a$10$2k7tZvyi3POrb7ypUoHo8uJ7Uts8nSG9TIBgB.FC/Rix2HSWJDde. | OFFICER

$2a is the BCrypt version, $10 the cost (2¹⁰ rounds), then 22 characters of salt and 31 of hash.

false 401 true

The supplier they created still says createdBy: "officer". Nothing was lost. To check all of this in one go, run the Lesson 2 smoke test.

TEST ITUsers now come from the database

SecurityTest's officer and approver no longer exist, so the test inserts them. @Transactional rolls them back after every test.

SecurityTest.java: add
@Autowired AppUserRepository users;
@Autowired PasswordEncoder encoder;

@BeforeEach
void createUsers() {
    users.save(new AppUser("officer", encoder.encode("officer-pass"), "Test Officer", Set.of(Role.OFFICER)));
    users.save(new AppUser("approver", encoder.encode("approver-pass"), "Test Approver", Set.of(Role.APPROVER)));
}
Why this worksMockMvc runs on the test's thread. The user inserted in @BeforeEach isn't committed, but DatabaseUserDetailsService runs in the same transaction and can see it.

@Test
void admin_creates_a_user_and_the_response_has_no_password() throws Exception {
    mvc.perform(post("/api/v1/users").with(httpBasic("admin", "admin-pass"))
            .contentType(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON).content(NEW_USER))
        .andExpect(status().isCreated())
        .andExpect(jsonPath("$.username").value("asha.juma"))

        .andExpect(jsonPath("$.passwordHash").doesNotExist());

void a_disabled_user_can_no_longer_log_in() throws Exception {

    mvc.perform(patch("/api/v1/users/{id}/enabled", officer.getId())
            .with(httpBasic("admin", "admin-pass"))
            .contentType(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON).content("{ \"enabled\": false }"))

MISTAKESWhat usually goes wrong

Every login returns 401 after the upgrade

Look for "Found 2 UserDetailsService beans" in the log: the in-memory bean from Lesson 1 is still there. Otherwise check that app_user has rows.

LazyInitializationException on login

Add @Transactional(readOnly = true) to loadUserByUsername. With open-in-view: false, nothing keeps the session open for you.

I changed ADMIN_PASSWORD but the old password still works

Correct behaviour. The initializer only runs while app_user is empty. Change the password through the API (Exercise 3).

"There is no PasswordEncoder mapped for the id null"

A row has a hash without {bcrypt}, usually inserted by hand in psql. Always create users through passwordEncoder.encode(...).

created_by is always null

@EnableJpaAuditing is missing auditorAwareRef = "currentUser", or the entity doesn't extend Auditable. Rows created before V5 stay null: nobody knows who made them.

"Migration checksum mismatch for migration version 4"

You edited V4 after it ran. Put the change in a new V6__...sql.

Tests fail with duplicate key … uk_app_user_username

The tests and the running app share a database, so an officer created with curl collides with the test's own. Keep TEST_DB_URL on pmis_test, never pmis.

EXERCISESNow you try

1 · Read the tables Warm-up

Create an approver juma through the API. In psql: what is the cost factor of Juma's hash? Create a second user with the same password: are the hashes equal? Who created Juma's account?

Solution

Cost is 10 (after the second $). The hashes differ, because each has its own random salt. created_by is admin, filled in by AuditorAware.

2 · Break it on purpose Understanding

Swap the two new rules so /api/v1/users/** comes before /api/v1/users/me. Run mvn test. Which tests fail?

Solution

Two tests fail, both with 403 instead of 200: any_user_can_ask_who_they_are and a_new_user_can_log_in_straight_away. /me also matches /users/**, so only admins can reach it.

3 · Change my own password Core

Add PUT /api/v1/users/me/password with { "currentPassword", "newPassword" }. Wrong current password returns 422; success returns 204, the old password stops working and the new one works.

Solution
// dto/PasswordChangeRequest.java
public record PasswordChangeRequest(
        @NotBlank(message = "current password is required")
        String currentPassword,

        @NotBlank(message = "new password is required")
        @Size(min = 8, max = 72, message = "password must be 8-72 characters")
        String newPassword
) { }

// UserService
@Transactional
public void changePassword(String username, PasswordChangeRequest request) {
    AppUser user = users.findByUsername(username)
            .orElseThrow(() -> new ResourceNotFoundException("User", username));
    // Ask for the old password: a stolen, still-logged-in session must not be enough.
    if (!passwordEncoder.matches(request.currentPassword(), user.getPasswordHash())) {
        throw new BusinessRuleException("Current password is incorrect");
    }
    user.setPasswordHash(passwordEncoder.encode(request.newPassword()));
}

// UserController
@PutMapping("/me/password")
@ResponseStatus(HttpStatus.NO_CONTENT)
public void changePassword(@AuthenticationPrincipal UserDetails principal,
                           @Valid @RequestBody PasswordChangeRequest request) {
    service.changePassword(principal.getUsername(), request);
}

// SecurityConfig: next to the /me rule, above /api/v1/users/**
.requestMatchers(HttpMethod.PUT, "/api/v1/users/me/password").authenticated()

The username comes from @AuthenticationPrincipal, never from the body or URL, so nobody can aim this at someone else's account.

try it
curl -s -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code}\n' -u 'officer:{{OFFICER}}' -X PUT {{BASE}}/api/v1/users/me/password \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"currentPassword":"{{OFFICER}}","newPassword":"brand-new-pass"}'     # 204

4 · Who really raised this requisition? Core + discussion

Add createdBy and updatedBy to RequisitionResponse. Create a requisition as officer with "requestedBy": "Director General". Which field would an auditor trust?

Solution

Add the two fields after updatedAt and pass r.getCreatedBy(), r.getUpdatedBy() in from(). The response shows requestedBy: "Director General" but createdBy: "officer". Trust createdBy: it comes from the login. requestedBy is whatever someone typed, so never use it for a security decision.

5 · An admin can't lock themselves out Stretch

Disabling your own account returns 422 with "You cannot disable your own account".

Solution
// UserController
@PatchMapping("/{id}/enabled")
public UserResponse setEnabled(@PathVariable UUID id,
                               @Valid @RequestBody UserEnabledRequest request,
                               @AuthenticationPrincipal UserDetails principal) {
    return service.setEnabled(id, request.enabled(), principal.getUsername());
}

// UserService
@Transactional
public UserResponse setEnabled(UUID id, boolean enabled, String currentUsername) {
    AppUser user = users.findById(id)
            .orElseThrow(() -> new ResourceNotFoundException("User", id));
    if (!enabled && user.getUsername().equals(currentUsername)) {
        throw new BusinessRuleException("You cannot disable your own account");
    }
    user.setEnabled(enabled);
    return UserResponse.from(user);
}

A business rule, so it lives in the service. Security rules decide which role may call an endpoint; business rules decide what that call may do.

QUIZCheck yourself

1. Which class compares the password from the Basic header with the stored hash?

Our service only loads the user and hash. The provider calls passwordEncoder.matches(...).

2. Why does an unknown username give the same 401 as a wrong password?

Different answers would let an attacker list valid usernames.

3. Where does supplier.created_by come from?

The client can't set it: SupplierRequest has no such field.

4. Why disable accounts instead of deleting them?

Procurement records are evidence.

5. After the first start you change ADMIN_PASSWORD and restart. What is the admin's password?

The initializer only runs while app_user is empty. From then on the password lives in the database.

6. Why is the table called app_user?

SELECT * FROM user returns the current database user, not your table.

SMOKE TESTCheck the whole lesson in one go

Creates officer and approver if missing, checks created_by, and disables and re-enables the officer. Save it as a file and run bash smoke.sh while the app is up. Each step prints PASS or FAIL with the status it got.

Needs only the admin password to start: it creates officer and approver with the passwords in Your setup. Safe to run again (existing accounts return 409). Adds one "Smoke Test Supplier" per run.

smoke-lesson2.sh
#!/usr/bin/env bash
# Lesson 2 smoke test: users in the database, and who did what.
# Creates one "Smoke Test Supplier" per run in the database you point it at.
BASE={{BASE}}
ADMIN='admin:{{ADMIN}}'
OFFICER='officer:{{OFFICER}}'
APPROVER='approver:{{APPROVER}}'
JSON='Content-Type: application/json'
ID=99999999-9999-9999-9999-999999999999

check() {   # check <expected status, e.g. 200 or "201|409"> <description> <curl args...>
  local want=$1 desc=$2; shift 2
  local got
  got=$(curl -s -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code}' "$@")
  if echo "$got" | grep -qE "^($want)$"; then echo "PASS  $got  $desc"; else echo "FAIL  $got  $desc (expected $want)"; fi
}

echo "--- accounts"
check 200       "admin logs in from app_user"              -u "$ADMIN" "$BASE/api/v1/users/me"
check "201|409" "admin creates officer (409: exists)"      -u "$ADMIN" -X POST -H "$JSON" \
      -d '{"username":"officer","password":"{{OFFICER}}","fullName":"Neema Said","roles":["OFFICER"]}' "$BASE/api/v1/users"
check "201|409" "admin creates approver (409: exists)"     -u "$ADMIN" -X POST -H "$JSON" \
      -d '{"username":"approver","password":"{{APPROVER}}","fullName":"Juma Ali","roles":["APPROVER"]}' "$BASE/api/v1/users"
check 200       "officer logs in"                          -u "$OFFICER" "$BASE/api/v1/users/me"
check 403       "officer cannot list users"                -u "$OFFICER" "$BASE/api/v1/users"
check 404       "approver can approve"                     -u "$APPROVER" -X POST "$BASE/api/v1/requisitions/$ID/approve"

echo "--- who did it"
TIN=$(printf '%03d-%03d-%03d' $((RANDOM % 1000)) $((RANDOM % 1000)) $((RANDOM % 1000)))
BODY=$(curl -s -u "$OFFICER" -X POST -H "$JSON" \
  -d "{\"name\":\"Smoke Test Supplier $TIN\",\"tin\":\"$TIN\",\"registrationNumber\":\"SMOKE-$TIN\",\"category\":\"GOODS\",\"email\":\"smoke@example.co.tz\"}" \
  "$BASE/api/v1/suppliers")
if echo "$BODY" | grep -q '"createdBy":"officer"'; then echo "PASS  201  new supplier has createdBy = officer"
else echo "FAIL       new supplier createdBy: $BODY"; fi

echo "--- disable and enable"
OFFICER_ID=$(curl -s -u "$ADMIN" "$BASE/api/v1/users" | grep -o '"id":"[^"]*","username":"officer"' | cut -d'"' -f4)
check 200 "admin disables officer"       -u "$ADMIN" -X PATCH -H "$JSON" -d '{"enabled":false}' "$BASE/api/v1/users/$OFFICER_ID/enabled"
check 401 "disabled officer is refused"  -u "$OFFICER" "$BASE/api/v1/suppliers"
check 200 "admin enables officer again"  -u "$ADMIN" -X PATCH -H "$JSON" -d '{"enabled":true}' "$BASE/api/v1/users/$OFFICER_ID/enabled"
check 200 "officer is back"              -u "$OFFICER" "$BASE/api/v1/suppliers"
--- accounts PASS 200 admin logs in from app_user PASS 201 admin creates officer (409: exists) PASS 201 admin creates approver (409: exists) PASS 200 officer logs in PASS 403 officer cannot list users PASS 404 approver can approve --- who did it PASS 201 new supplier has createdBy = officer --- disable and enable PASS 200 admin disables officer PASS 401 disabled officer is refused PASS 200 admin enables officer again PASS 200 officer is back
If officer logs in fails with 401The officer account already exists with a different password, so the create step returned 409 and left it unchanged. Put that password in Your setup, or disable the old account and create a new one.

Lesson 3 · 2½ hours

Tokens and JWT

Show your password once, get a pass, show the pass from then on.

ProjectPIS project Markdowndocs/lessons/03-jwt.md Check itStep by step · Smoke test

GOALSBy the end of this lesson you can…

  • Explain why a front end wants tokens instead of sending a password on every request.
  • Take a JWT apart: header, payload, signature. Say what each proves, and what it doesn't hide.
  • Issue a signed token at a login endpoint with JwtEncoder.
  • Accept tokens with oauth2ResourceServer().jwt() and map a roles claim to hasRole(...).
  • Explain why a token keeps working after its account is disabled, and what short expiry buys you.
  • Test with a real login, a forged token, an expired token, and jwt().
TimeActivity
0–20Sessions vs tokens; anatomy of a JWT; signing vs encrypting
20–30Request flow: log in once, then Bearer on every request
30–80Build it: dependency, secret, encoder and decoder, login endpoint, wiring
80–100Try it live: log in, decode the token, forge one, watch it fail
100–120The trade-off: a token outlives a disabled account
120–150Tests, exercises, quiz

CONCEPTSPassword once, token after

HTTP Basic (Lessons 1–2)Bearer token (Lesson 3)
The password travels on every requestThe password travels once, at login
A browser front end must hold on to the passwordThe front end holds a token that expires by itself
BCrypt on every request (slow by design: tens of milliseconds)An HMAC signature check (microseconds)
No logout: the browser caches credentialsThrow the token away; it also dies at exp
Looks the user up in app_user every timeTrusts what is signed in the token: no database lookup

The last row is both the big advantage and the big catch. See the trade-off.

Anatomy of a JWT

Three base64url strings joined by dots. A real one from PIS, taken apart:

eyJhbGciOiJIUzI1NiJ9 → {"alg":"HS256"} . eyJpc3MiOiJwaXMiLCJzdWIiOiJvZmZpY2VyIiwiZXhwIjoxNzkwMjUxNzIxLCJpYXQiOjE3OTAyNDk5MjEsInJvbGVzIjpbIk9GRklDRVIiXX0 → {"iss":"pis","sub":"officer","exp":1790251721, "iat":1790249921,"roles":["OFFICER"]} . <43 characters> → HMAC-SHA256(header + "." + payload, JWT_SECRET)
ClaimMeaningSet to
issIssuer: who made the token"pis"
subSubject: who the token is aboutthe username
iatIssued at (seconds since 1970)now
expExpires atnow + 30 minutes
rolesOur own claimthe user's roles at login time
Signed, not encryptedAnyone holding a token can read the payload. It is as readable as a Basic header. Never put a password, national ID or salary in a claim. What the signature does guarantee: nobody without JWT_SECRET can change a character without the server noticing. Change OFFICER to ADMIN and you get 401, not admin.

Who holds the secret?

HS256 is symmetric: the same JWT_SECRET signs at login and checks on every request. That's fine while one app does both. When a separate service or an identity provider (Keycloak, Entra ID) issues tokens, you switch to RS256: the issuer signs with a private key, and APIs check with the public key. The resource-server code stays almost the same.

Stateless: the server remembers nothing

With STATELESS sessions (set since Lesson 1), any server behind a load balancer can check any token, and there is nothing to log out on the server. What the token says is what the server believes, until exp.

REQUEST FLOWLog in once, Bearer after

sequenceDiagram
    participant C as Client
    participant L as AuthController / TokenService
    participant M as AuthenticationManager
    participant F as BearerTokenAuthenticationFilter
    participant D as JwtDecoder
    participant API as SupplierController
    Note over C,M: Once: log in with a password
    C->>L: POST /api/v1/auth/login {username, password}
    L->>M: authenticate(username, password)
    M-->>L: OK (DatabaseUserDetailsService + BCrypt)
    L-->>C: 200 {accessToken, tokenType Bearer, expiresIn 1800}
    Note over C,API: Every later request: no password, no database lookup
    C->>F: GET /api/v1/suppliers + Authorization: Bearer eyJ...
    F->>D: decode(token)
    D->>D: check HS256 signature and exp
    alt signature wrong, expired, or garbage
        D-->>C: 401 + WWW-Authenticate: Bearer error="invalid_token"
    else valid
        D-->>F: Jwt sub=officer, roles=[OFFICER] to ROLE_OFFICER
        F->>API: same hasRole(...) rules as before
        API-->>C: 200
    end

BUILD ITSeven steps

1 · The dependency

pom.xml, next to spring-boot-starter-security
<dependency>
    <groupId>org.springframework.boot</groupId>
    <artifactId>spring-boot-starter-oauth2-resource-server</artifactId>
</dependency>

This brings Spring's JWT support and the Nimbus crypto library. No other JWT library and no hand-written JwtFilter. Tutorials using jjwt rebuild what Spring already ships.

2 · The secret and the expiry

application.yml
pis:
  security:
    admin-password: ${ADMIN_PASSWORD}
    jwt:
      # HS256 needs at least 32 bytes. Generate one with:  openssl rand -base64 32
      secret: ${JWT_SECRET}
      expiry: 30m
terminal: generate a secret and add it to .env
echo "JWT_SECRET=$(openssl rand -base64 32)" >> .env
src/test/resources/application-test.yml
pis:
  security:
    admin-password: admin-pass
    jwt:
      secret: test-only-secret-that-is-at-least-32-bytes-long
      expiry: 30m
More powerful than the admin passwordWhoever has JWT_SECRET can mint a token for any user with any role. Keep it in .env only, never git, and use a different value on every server.
config/JwtProperties.java
/** pis.security.jwt.* bound and type-checked at startup. expiry accepts "30m", "8h" or "PT30M". */
@ConfigurationProperties(prefix = "pis.security.jwt")
public record JwtProperties(String secret, Duration expiry) { }

3 · Encoder, decoder, and the roles claim

config/JwtConfig.java
/**
 * One secret key, used twice: the encoder signs tokens at login, the decoder
 * checks the signature on every request. It never leaves the server.
 */
@Configuration
@EnableConfigurationProperties(JwtProperties.class)
public class JwtConfig {

    @Bean
    public SecretKey jwtSigningKey(JwtProperties properties) {
        byte[] bytes = properties.secret().getBytes(StandardCharsets.UTF_8);
        if (bytes.length < 32) {
            // HS256 is only as strong as its key: 256 bits minimum.
            throw new IllegalStateException("JWT_SECRET must be at least 32 bytes; got " + bytes.length);
        }
        return new SecretKeySpec(bytes, "HmacSHA256");
    }

    @Bean
    public JwtEncoder jwtEncoder(SecretKey jwtSigningKey) {
        return new NimbusJwtEncoder(new ImmutableSecret<>(jwtSigningKey));
    }

    /** Checks signature and expiry on every request. A token that fails either is a 401. */
    @Bean
    public JwtDecoder jwtDecoder(SecretKey jwtSigningKey) {
        return NimbusJwtDecoder.withSecretKey(jwtSigningKey)
                .macAlgorithm(MacAlgorithm.HS256)
                .build();
    }

    /**
     * Turns the token's "roles": ["OFFICER"] claim into the authority ROLE_OFFICER,
     * so every hasRole(...) rule from Lesson 1 works unchanged.
     */
    @Bean
    public JwtAuthenticationConverter jwtAuthenticationConverter() {
        JwtGrantedAuthoritiesConverter roles = new JwtGrantedAuthoritiesConverter();
        roles.setAuthoritiesClaimName("roles");
        roles.setAuthorityPrefix("ROLE_");

        JwtAuthenticationConverter converter = new JwtAuthenticationConverter();
        converter.setJwtGrantedAuthoritiesConverter(roles);
        return converter;
    }
}
Why the converter?By default Spring reads a scope claim and prefixes it SCOPE_, the OAuth 2 convention. No hasRole rule would ever match. Configure it once here and the rules from Lesson 1 don't change.

4 · The login endpoint

dto/LoginRequest.java · dto/TokenResponse.java
public record LoginRequest(
        @NotBlank(message = "username is required") String username,
        @NotBlank(message = "password is required") String password
) { }

/** Field names follow OAuth 2 (RFC 6749 §5.1), so any client library understands them. */
public record TokenResponse(
        String accessToken,
        String tokenType,
        long expiresIn
) { }
service/TokenService.java
@RequiredArgsConstructor
@Service
public class TokenService {

    private final AuthenticationManager authenticationManager;
    private final JwtEncoder jwtEncoder;
    private final JwtProperties properties;

    public TokenResponse login(String username, String password) {
        // The same check as HTTP Basic: DatabaseUserDetailsService + BCrypt.
        // Wrong password, unknown user or disabled account throws AuthenticationException.
        Authentication auth = authenticationManager.authenticate(
                UsernamePasswordAuthenticationToken.unauthenticated(username, password));

        List<String> roles = auth.getAuthorities().stream()
                .map(GrantedAuthority::getAuthority)
                .map(authority -> authority.replaceFirst("^ROLE_", ""))
                .toList();

        Instant now = Instant.now();
        JwtClaimsSet claims = JwtClaimsSet.builder()
                .issuer("pis")
                .subject(auth.getName())      // becomes Authentication.getName() on later requests
                .issuedAt(now)
                .expiresAt(now.plus(properties.expiry()))
                .claim("roles", roles)
                .build();

        // Without an explicit header Spring would pick RS256, which needs a private key we don't have.
        JwsHeader header = JwsHeader.with(MacAlgorithm.HS256).build();
        String token = jwtEncoder.encode(JwtEncoderParameters.from(header, claims)).getTokenValue();

        return new TokenResponse(token, "Bearer", properties.expiry().toSeconds());
    }
}
controller/AuthController.java
@Tag(name = "Authentication", description = "Exchange a username and password for a token")
@RequiredArgsConstructor
@RestController
@RequestMapping("/api/v1/auth")
public class AuthController {

    private final TokenService tokenService;

    @PostMapping("/login")
    public TokenResponse login(@Valid @RequestBody LoginRequest request) {
        return tokenService.login(request.username(), request.password());
    }
}

5 · Wire it into SecurityConfig

config/SecurityConfig.java: three additions
private final JwtAuthenticationConverter jwtAuthenticationConverter;   // new field

// inside authorizeHttpRequests, right after the permitAll() line:
// You can't need a token to get a token.
.requestMatchers(HttpMethod.POST, "/api/v1/auth/login").permitAll()

// after .httpBasic(...):
// Reads "Authorization: Bearer ...", checks signature and expiry with the
// JwtDecoder bean, and turns the roles claim into ROLE_ authorities.
.oauth2ResourceServer(jwt -> jwt
        .jwt(j -> j.jwtAuthenticationConverter(jwtAuthenticationConverter))
        .authenticationEntryPoint(problemHandler)   // bad or expired token
        .accessDeniedHandler(problemHandler))

// new bean: the same AuthenticationManager HTTP Basic uses, for TokenService
@Bean
public AuthenticationManager authenticationManager(AuthenticationConfiguration config) throws Exception {
    return config.getAuthenticationManager();
}

6 · Three small fixes that tokens force on us

a) A failed login must be 401, not 500. In Lesson 1 a wrong password was rejected in a filter. Now authenticate(...) runs inside a controller, so the exception lands in GlobalExceptionHandler and would fall through to the catch-all 500.

exception/GlobalExceptionHandler.java: above the Exception handler
/** A failed login. One message for wrong password, unknown user and disabled account alike. */
@ExceptionHandler(AuthenticationException.class)
public ProblemDetail onLoginFailed(AuthenticationException ex) {
    return problem(HttpStatus.UNAUTHORIZED, "Login failed",
            "Invalid username or password", "login-failed");
}

b) A bad token should say so (RFC 6750), so the client knows to log in again.

config/ProblemDetailSecurityHandler.java: start of commence(...)
String authorization = request.getHeader(HttpHeaders.AUTHORIZATION);
if (authorization != null && authorization.startsWith("Bearer ")) {
    response.setHeader(HttpHeaders.WWW_AUTHENTICATE, "Bearer error=\"invalid_token\"");
    write(response, HttpStatus.UNAUTHORIZED, "Invalid token",
            "The token is invalid or has expired. Log in again at POST /api/v1/auth/login",
            "invalid-token");
    return;
}
// ... the Basic branch from Lesson 1 continues unchanged

c) With a token the principal is a Jwt, not a UserDetails. @AuthenticationPrincipal UserDetails would be null, and /me would throw a NullPointerException.

controller/UserController.java
@GetMapping("/me")
public UserResponse me(Authentication authentication) {
    // getName() works for Basic and Bearer alike: for a token it is the "sub" claim.
    return service.findByUsername(authentication.getName());
}

AuditorAware from Lesson 2 already uses getName(), so created_by works with tokens without any change.

7 · Swagger UI

config/OpenApiConfig.java
.components(new Components()
        .addSecuritySchemes("bearerAuth", new SecurityScheme()
                .type(SecurityScheme.Type.HTTP).scheme("bearer").bearerFormat("JWT"))
        .addSecuritySchemes("basicAuth", new SecurityScheme()
                .type(SecurityScheme.Type.HTTP).scheme("basic")))
.addSecurityItem(new SecurityRequirement().addList("bearerAuth"))
.addSecurityItem(new SecurityRequirement().addList("basicAuth"));

In Swagger UI: call Authentication → Log in, copy accessToken, click Authorize and paste it into bearerAuth. Don't type the word Bearer; Swagger adds it.

TRY ITLive demo with curl

Add JWT_SECRET to .env, start the app, and make sure officer and approver exist (the Lesson 2 smoke test creates them).

Log in

terminal
curl -s -X POST {{BASE}}/api/v1/auth/login \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"username":"officer","password":"{{OFFICER}}"}' | jq
200 { "accessToken": "eyJhbGciOiJIUzI1NiJ9.eyJpc3MiOiJwaXMiLCJzdWIiOiJvZmZpY2VyIi…", "tokenType": "Bearer", "expiresIn": 1800 }

Keep it in a shell variable, then read it: anyone can

terminal
TOKEN=$(curl -s -X POST {{BASE}}/api/v1/auth/login \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"username":"officer","password":"{{OFFICER}}"}' | jq -r .accessToken)

echo "$TOKEN" | jq -R 'split(".") | .[1] | gsub("-";"+") | gsub("_";"/") | @base64d | fromjson | .exp |= todate'
{ "iss": "pis", "sub": "officer", "exp": "2026-09-24T12:09:03Z", "iat": 1790249943, "roles": ["OFFICER"] }

Or paste it into jwt.io. It reads everything, but shows invalid signature until you give it the secret.

Use it

terminal
curl -s -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code}\n' -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" {{BASE}}/api/v1/suppliers
curl -s -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" {{BASE}}/api/v1/users/me | jq .username
curl -s -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code}\n' -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" -X POST \
  {{BASE}}/api/v1/requisitions/99999999-9999-9999-9999-999999999999/approve
200 "officer" 403 the role comes from inside the token

A bad token

terminal
curl -s -i -H "Authorization: Bearer not.a.token" {{BASE}}/api/v1/suppliers
401 WWW-Authenticate: Bearer error="invalid_token" {"type":"https://hmy.co.tz/problems/invalid-token","title":"Invalid token", "detail":"The token is invalid or has expired. Log in again at POST /api/v1/auth/login",...}

Forge a token: officer → ADMIN

The moment the lesson is built around. The payload is readable and editable, so let the class try to promote themselves:

terminal (plain bash, no jq needed)
b64url_decode() { local s; s=$(printf '%s' "$1" | tr '_-' '/+'); while [ $(( ${#s} % 4 )) -ne 0 ]; do s="$s="; done; printf '%s' "$s" | base64 -d; }
b64url_encode() { base64 | tr '/+' '_-' | tr -d '=\n'; }

HEADER=$(echo "$TOKEN" | cut -d. -f1)
PAYLOAD=$(b64url_decode "$(echo "$TOKEN" | cut -d. -f2)")
SIGNATURE=$(echo "$TOKEN" | cut -d. -f3)
echo "$PAYLOAD"
FORGED="$HEADER.$(printf '%s' "${PAYLOAD/OFFICER/ADMIN}" | b64url_encode).$SIGNATURE"

curl -s -o /dev/null -w 'real token:   %{http_code}\n' -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" -X DELETE \
  {{BASE}}/api/v1/suppliers/99999999-9999-9999-9999-999999999999
curl -s -o /dev/null -w 'forged token: %{http_code}\n' -H "Authorization: Bearer $FORGED" -X DELETE \
  {{BASE}}/api/v1/suppliers/99999999-9999-9999-9999-999999999999
{"iss":"pis","sub":"officer",...,"roles":["OFFICER"]} real token: 403 officer can't delete forged token: 401 signature doesn't match the edited payload

Not 404 (which would mean "got in as ADMIN"), and not even 403. The server doesn't trust a single claim in a token whose signature is wrong.

THE TRADE-OFFA token outlives a disabled account

terminal (uses $TOKEN from above)
ADMIN_TOKEN=$(curl -s -X POST {{BASE}}/api/v1/auth/login -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"username":"admin","password":"{{ADMIN}}"}' | jq -r .accessToken)
ID=$(curl -s -H "Authorization: Bearer $ADMIN_TOKEN" {{BASE}}/api/v1/users | jq -r '.[] | select(.username=="officer") | .id')

curl -s -H "Authorization: Bearer $ADMIN_TOKEN" -X PATCH {{BASE}}/api/v1/users/$ID/enabled \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d '{"enabled":false}' | jq .enabled

curl -s -o /dev/null -w 'new login: %{http_code}\n' -X POST {{BASE}}/api/v1/auth/login \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d '{"username":"officer","password":"{{OFFICER}}"}'
curl -s -o /dev/null -w 'Basic:     %{http_code}\n' -u 'officer:{{OFFICER}}' {{BASE}}/api/v1/suppliers
curl -s -o /dev/null -w 'old token: %{http_code}\n' -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" {{BASE}}/api/v1/suppliers

curl -s -H "Authorization: Bearer $ADMIN_TOKEN" -X PATCH {{BASE}}/api/v1/users/$ID/enabled \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d '{"enabled":true}' | jq .enabled
false new login: 401 Basic: 401 old token: 200 (!) true

The disabled officer can't log in and can't use Basic, but the token they already hold works until it expires. Nothing looks at app_user when a token arrives. That's the "no database lookup" advantage, seen from the other side.

MitigationCost
Short expiry (we use 30 min; high-security systems often use 5)Users log in more often, unless you add refresh tokens
Check the account on every request (Exercise 5)One database read per request: you've given up the main benefit
A deny-list of revoked token IDs (jti)A shared store (Redis) every server must check

For PIS, 30 minutes means a dismissed employee has at most half an hour. Ask the class: is that acceptable for a procurement system?
